**FINDING A: `addr & 4 = 1` is not enumerated for re-arm.** A 4-byte write at offset 0x0c (e.g. `sw 0x0c`) takes the `relative_addr & 4` true branch in the W arm; a 4-byte read at 0x0c takes the `(int32_t)odata` (sign-extended low half) branch. On R it covers `addr & 4 = 0` (lw 0x34, lw 0x30, lw 0x04, lw 0x00, lw 0x10, lw 0x18) but no entry for `addr & 4 = 1`.

Whether this is a gap depends on what a re-arm mutant looks like. A `d->warned = 0;` placed inside the `if (relative_addr & 4)` arm of the W path would only fire on a 4-byte write at an odd offset — and the KINDS tuple has no such access. **An `rearm_addr4` mutant (insertion inside the `if (relative_addr & 4)` block of the W arm) would NOT be caught by the re-arm session.**

But — and this is the careful read — is the `rearm_addr4` mutant equivalent? It only fires when a 4W hits an odd address in CRIME. The window's labels are 0x00, 0x08, 0x10, 0x18, 0x30, 0x38, all multiples of 8. A 4W at 0x04, 0x0c, 0x14, 0x1c, 0x34, 0x3c would hit it. The default arm is reached at all of those except 0x34 (DOG register), 0x3c — wait, 0x34 is `(0x34/8) = 6`, so `d->reg[6]`. The function's 4W at 0x34 writes the LOW half of register 6 (DOG register, since CRIME_DOG/8 = 6). So 0x34 is also `addr & 4 = 0`. The `addr & 4 = 1` lane for W is reached only at unaligned addresses inside the window: 0x04, 0x0c, 0x14, 0x1c, 0x24, 0x2c, 0x34's HIGH half is at 0x34, **no wait**, `0x34 & 4 = 4` is true. So `sw 0x34` IS `addr & 4 = 1`!

Re-reading the source:
```c
} else if (len == 4) {
    if (writeflag == MEM_WRITE) {
        if (relative_addr & 4) {
            d->reg[relative_addr / 8] &= ~0xffffffffULL;
            d->reg[relative_addr / 8] |= (uint32_t)idata;     // low half
        } else {
            d->reg[relative_addr / 8] &= 0xffffffffULL;
            d->reg[relative_addr / 8] |= (uint64_t)(idata << 32ULL);  // high half
        }
    } else {
        odata = d->reg[relative_addr / 8];
        if (relative_addr & 4)
            odata = (int32_t)odata;                            // low half, sign-extended
        else
            odata = (int32_t)(odata >> 32);                    // high half, sign-extended
    }
}
```

So `sw 0x34`: `0x34 & 4 = 4` true → writes the **low half** of register `(0x34)/8 = 6` (CRIME_DOG).
`sw 0x30`: `0x30 & 4 = 0` false → writes the **high half** of register `(0x30)/8 = 6` (CRIME_DOG).

Both `sw 0x30` and `sw 0x34` are W accesses to register 6. They cover BOTH `(addr & 4)` values for W to a single register. ✓

For R: `lw 0x34` reads low half (sign-extended) of reg 6; `lw 0x30` reads high half of reg 6. Both addr&4 values for R to a single register. ✓

So **my finding A is wrong** — the KINDS tuple does cover both `(addr & 4)` values for len 4, by hitting both halves of register 6 (DOG). The brief is correct.

This seeds register `(0x40)/8 = 8` to `0x0123456789abcdef`. `0x40 & 4 = 0`. The mutant `land_b4clr` is `if (writeflag == MEM_WRITE && !(relative_addr & 0x10)) d->reg[relative_addr / 8] = idata;` — keyed on **bit 4 clear** (mask 0x10). **FINDING B confirmed: the `lo keeps` row does NOT actually exercise the `land_b4clr` mutant's keying condition.** The mutant key is "bit 4 clear" (mask 0x10); the test stores are at 0x47 and 0x44, which both have bit 4 SET (0x10). The store at +0x40 (the seed) has bit 4 clear, but that's an ACCEPTED `sd`, not a refused store. The refused stores are `sb 0x47, sh 0x44` — both bit 4 SET.

**The `land_b4clr` mutant as written in POSTTABLE 7 (`g("if (writeflag == MEM_WRITE && !(relative_addr & 0x10)) d->reg[relative_addr / 8] = idata;")`) would NOT fire on the refused stores in the `lo keeps` session.** It would fire on the accepted `sd 0x40`, which would then overwrite the seed. But the `lo keeps` row checks `s2` (the `ld 0x40` after the refused stores), and if the mutant fired on the accepted `sd`, then `s2 = idata` from `sd` = `0x0123456789abcdef`. The expected value is `0x123456789abcdef` (which I assume is the byte-ordering of the seed). If the mutant fires on `sd` and writes `idata` directly to the register, the register still has `0x0123456789abcdef`, so `s2` reads the same. **The mutant is silent on this row either way.**

This is a real defect. The `lo keeps` row does not detect `land_b4clr` because:
1. The refused stores (the would-be landing sites) have bit 4 SET, not clear, so the mutant's predicate `!(addr & 0x10)` is FALSE for them.

Actually wait — `sh` at 0x40 writes the halfword at 0x40, which is the LOW half of register 8 (since 0x40/8 = 8). And 0x40 has bit 4 clear. The diff has `sh 0x44` (bit 4 SET, mutant silent) instead of `sh 0x40` (bit 4 clear, mutant fires). **This is a real floor gap.** The `lo keeps` row is supposed to detect `land_b4clr`; it does not.

Let me be careful. 0x40 = `0100 0000`. Bit 4 (the 0x10 position) is 0. So 0x40 has bit 4 clear.
0x44 = `0100 0100`. Bit 4 is 1. Bit 4 set.

0x40 in hex = 64 in decimal. Binary: `0110 0100`. Wait, no. 0x40 = 4*16 + 0 = 64. 64 in binary = `0100 0000` (8 bits). The bits from MSB to LSB: bit 7=0, bit 6=1, bit 5=0, bit 4=0, bit 3=0, bit 2=0, bit 1=0, bit 0=0. So 0x40 has bit 4 = 0 (clear). ✓

0x44 = 68 = `0100 0100`. Bit 4 = 1 (set).

So 0x47 and 0x44 both have bit 4 SET. The `lo keeps` row's refused stores are at bit-4-SET addresses. The mutant key is bit-4-CLEAR. **The row does not exercise the mutant.**

To exercise it, the refused stores should be at addresses with bit 4 clear: e.g. `sb 0x43` (67 = `0100 0011`, bit 4 clear), `sh 0x42` (66 = `0100 0010`, bit 4 clear), or `sb 0x41`, `sh 0x40`.

The switch doesn't have a case for 8 (the cases are 0, 1, 2, 3, 6, 7 — REV, CONTROL, INTSTAT, INTMASK, DOG, TIME). So 0x43 hits the default arm. ✓

The diff's `sh 0x44` is ALSO in the default arm (register 8, no case 8). But it's at a bit-4-set address, so the mutant ignores it.

**This is a genuine floor gap.** The `lo keeps` row as written does not catch `land_b4clr`. POSTTABLE 7's claim that it does is either a measurement artifact (the mutant failed to compile, or the row failed for an unrelated reason) or a check_pred miscount.

I cannot verify POSTTABLE's check_pred from the packet — I can only see that the row's predicate (address bits) and the mutant's predicate (address bits) don't match. **Not checkable from the packet: whether POSTTABLE's check_pred actually ran the mutant binary against the row, or whether it counted a different red.**

But the structural defect is clear: the row was MEANT to catch a landing keyed on bit-4-clear, and the addresses chosen have bit-4-set.

---

## 2. Is there a floor gap in the 131-row test?

**YES. GAP: `lo keeps` does not catch `land_b4clr`.** The smallest source edit to `dev_sgi_ip32.c` that reproduces this gap:

In the decline path (the `else` branch of the width switch, just before `goto crime_declined;`), insert:
```c
if (writeflag == MEM_WRITE && !(relative_addr & 0x10))
    d->reg[relative_addr / 8] = idata;
```

Expected catch: `lo keeps` (refused sb +0x47, sh +0x44 leave +0x40 seeded). Why it cannot: the refused stores are at 0x47 and 0x44, both of which have bit 4 (0x10) SET, so the mutant's predicate `!(addr & 0x10)` evaluates to FALSE for both. The mutant never fires on the refused stores; it would only fire on the accepted `sd 0x40` (which writes the same seed value), so the read-back `ld 0x40` sees the same value with or without the mutant. The row passes 131/131 against this mutant.

To fix: change the refused stores in the `lo keeps` session to addresses with bit 4 clear — e.g. `sb 0x43` and `sh 0x40` — while keeping the seed at an address with bit 4 clear (e.g. `sd 0x40`) and the read-back at the same address.

A second candidate gap (lower confidence): the KINDS tuple and prologue together cover `addr & 4` both ways for len-4 access, but the coverage is achieved by hitting register 6 (DOG) at offsets 0x30 and 0x34. A re-arm mutant placed inside the `if (relative_addr & 4)` arm of the W path would fire only on 4W at offsets with bit 2 set (0x04, 0x0c, 0x14, ...). The KINDS tuple has `sw 0x34` (bit 2 set, register 6 default arm — wait, 0x34 & 4 = 4, so bit 2 is set? 0x34 = `0011 0100`, bit 2 = 1). Yes. So `sw 0x34` does have bit 2 set, and a mutant in the `if (relative_addr & 4)` W arm would fire on it. ✓ — covered.

What about a re-arm mutant keyed on `(relative_addr & 4)` being FALSE in the W arm (i.e. in the `else` branch)? That fires on 4W at even-4 offsets: 0x00, 0x08, 0x10, 0x18, 0x20, 0x28, 0x30, 0x38. KINDS has `sw 0x08` and `sw 0x30`. ✓

So the KINDS coverage for re-arm looks complete.

## 3. Wrong statements

**In the brief / diff comment:** the comment on the new `lo keeps` rows says:
```python
#  a SEEDED slot with address bit 4 CLEAR (the Opus third-closing seat, measured: every refused store the rows read back
#  had bit 4 set, so a landing keyed on bit 4 clear passed); +0x40 reaches the default arm
```

The comment correctly identifies the intent (a refused store at a bit-4-clear address to catch a landing mutant keyed on bit-4-clear). But the actual addresses (`sb 0x47, sh 0x44`) have bit 4 SET, not clear. **The addresses contradict the comment.** This is a bug — either the addresses should be `sb 0x43, sh 0x40` (bit 4 clear), or the comment should say "bit 4 set" (in which case the row would not catch `land_b4clr`).

**In POSTTABLE 7:** "`r_land_b4clr, ... every one red, on the row the branch-derived enumeration predicted`" — this is either wrong (the row is actually green against this mutant) or relies on a measurement I cannot verify from the packet. The structural mismatch between the row's address bits and the mutant's keying condition makes the claim suspect.

**In the header:** the disclosed-debt bullet about "a refused sb/sh that LANDS only at CRIME_TIME stores <= 0xffff" — this is a separate, pre-existing debt, not affected by repair 9. Not relevant to this pass's gap.

## The Fix (the gold standard)

The fix introduces:
1. An `int warned` field with a `CRIME_W_LEN` bit
2. A `goto crime_declined;` to skip the register switch when width is not 4 or 8

The fix makes narrowed accesses (widths 1, 2) declined rather than crashing, with the announce being a fatal() that prints once.

## The Test's Contract (clause e)

10 numbered items cover:
1. The kill reinstated, or other host death at width arm
2. FAULT: returning 0 (bus error)

4. The latch (single-axis generating set, pre-set, test, suppress, etc.)

6. Accept set shrunk
7. Refused store that lands
8. State carried across calls (warm v0, warm once, re-arm, etc.)

### Question 1: Verify the derivation

Let me check each branch the derivation claims:

**Pre-set list derivation:**

Claim: "len 8 (W, R); len 4 (W, R) x (relative_addr & 4); every case label; REV's pc-window test (in, out); CONTROL's writeflag test (R, W) x its DOG test (set, clear) x its leftover test (nonzero, zero); the default arm (R, W)"

Let me check what each case label can do with accepted access:
- CRIME_REV: only has a pc-window test; doesn't matter direction (per codex: "the redirect branch"). So pc-window in/out covers both directions.
- CRIME_CONTROL: depends on writeflag. Inside write: DOG test, then if DOG clear, leftover test (the fatal). So W path has DOG set + leftover nonzero (which goes to fatal) and DOG clear + leftover nonzero, DOG clear + leftover zero.
  - Wait, looking at code: 
    ```c
    if (idata & CRIME_CONTROL_DOG_ENABLE) {
        idata &= ~CRIME_CONTROL_DOG_ENABLE;
    }
    if (idata & CRIME_CONTROL_HARD_RESET) {
        ...
    }
    idata &= ~CRIME_CONTROL_ENDIANESS;
    if (idata != 0)
        fatal("[ CRIME_CONTROL: unimplemented control 0x%016llx ]\n", ...);
    ```
  - So branches:
    - DOG bit set: takes the first branch (clears DOG)
    - HARD_RESET bit set: takes the second branch (stops CPUs)
    - After both clear, leftover is checked. If nonzero: fatal. If zero: no fatal.
  - So three branches: DOG-handling (set), HARD_RESET-handling (set, stops CPUs - disclosed), and leftover-nonzero (fatal) vs leftover-zero (no fatal).
  - Wait, can a single value trigger DOG AND have leftover nonzero? Yes - if value is DOG_ENABLE | something_else, after clearing DOG, leftover is something_else (nonzero).
  - Can a single value have leftover zero? Yes - just write 0, or just DOG_ENABLE (after clearing, leftover is 0).
  - But wait: HARD_RESET's true side stops CPUs and is unrowable. So we need to avoid HARD_RESET bit.
  - But - what about HARD_RESET being cleared too? Looking at the code, HARD_RESET is conditionally tested. If HARD_RESET is set, it stops CPUs. If HARD_RESET is not set, this branch isn't taken. So the value 0x800 is dangerous.
  - For an idata of 0x0201 (DOG_ENABLE | 0x0001): DOG_ENABLE branch taken (clears DOG), HARD_RESET branch not taken, ENDIANESS cleared, leftover = 0x0001 (nonzero), fatal.
  - For an idata of 0x0001 (no DOG, no HARD_RESET, no ENDIANESS, leftover 0x0001): no branch taken, leftover = 0x0001 (nonzero), fatal.
  - For an idata of 0 (no DOG, no HARD_RESET, no ENDIANESS, leftover 0): no branch taken, leftover = 0, no fatal.
  - The pre-set uses: 0x0201 (DOG set + nonzero leftover), 0x0001 (DOG clear + nonzero leftover), 0 (DOG clear + zero leftover). These are the three non-CPU-stopping branches. ✓
  - And the read side: "lw 0x08" - 4R at CONTROL. The CONTROL case on read does nothing (the if (writeflag == MEM_WRITE) check). ✓
- CRIME_INTSTAT/INTMASK: just call crime_interrupt_reassert(d). lw at +0x10 and +0x18 ✓
- CRIME_DOG: just `break;`. ld at +0x30 ✓ (and lb doesn't go there since len!=4, but for accepted it's 8R or 4R)
- CRIME_TIME: just call crime_update_crime_time(d). ld at +0x38 ✓
- default: debug(). sw/lw at +0x34 (4-byte path) ✓

0x30 = 0b110000. 0x30 & 4 = 0. Low half.
0x34 = 0b110100. 0x34 & 4 = 4. High half.
0x04 = 0b100. 0x04 & 4 = 4. High half.

The 131-row test verifies the behavior of the device under mutations. The pre-set list determines which access patterns the test exercises before the first refusal. The brief claims the pre-set list is "DERIVED FROM THE FUNCTION'S OWN BRANCHES".

Looking at the pre-set list and comparing to branches:

Function branches (for an accepted access):

- 4W with DOG clear + nonzero leftover: e.g., 0x0001 (no fatal bit, leftover = 0x0001, fatal)

```c

    idata &= ~CRIME_CONTROL_DOG_ENABLE;
}

    /* stops CPUs */
    idata &= ~CRIME_CONTROL_HARD_RESET;
}
idata &= ~CRIME_CONTROL_ENDIANESS;
if (idata != 0)
    fatal(...);
```

So if idata = 0x0200 (DOG_ENABLE only):
- First if taken (idata = 0)
- Second if not taken
- idata = 0
- if (idata != 0) not taken → no fatal

If idata = 0x0201 (DOG_ENABLE | 0x0001):
- First if taken (idata = 0x0001)
- Second if not taken
- idata = 0x0001
- if (idata != 0) taken → fatal

If idata = 0x0001 (no DOG):
- First if not taken
- Second if not taken
- idata = 0x0001
- if (idata != 0) taken → fatal

If idata = 0 (no DOG, no bits):
- First if not taken
- Second if not taken
- idata = 0
- if (idata != 0) not taken → no fatal

If idata = 0x0800 (HARD_RESET only, no DOG):
- First if not taken
- Second if taken (stops CPUs)
- idata = 0
- if (idata != 0) not taken → no fatal

If idata = 0x0900 (HARD_RESET | ENDIANESS):
- First if not taken (ENDIANESS is not DOG)
- Second if taken (stops CPUs)
- idata = 0
- if (idata != 0) not taken → no fatal

So the branches are:
- DOG_ENABLE set vs not (binary decision)
- HARD_RESET set vs not (binary decision; set stops CPUs)
- After clearing: ENDIANESS bit is always stripped
- leftover == 0 vs nonzero

The pre-set covers:
- DOG set, leftover nonzero (0x0201)
- DOG clear, leftover nonzero (0x0001)
- DOG clear, leftover zero (0)

Missing: DOG set, leftover zero (e.g., 0x0200).

This is potentially a gap. Could a mutation add `d->warned |= CRIME_W_LEN` ONLY in the DOG_ENABLE branch when leftover would be zero? That would catch: `if (idata & CRIME_CONTROL_DOG_ENABLE) d->warned |= CRIME_W_LEN; idata &= ~CRIME_CONTROL_DOG_ENABLE;`. This pre-sets the latch only when DOG is set.

Which adds a pre-set in the DOG_ENABLE branch. The Opus seat's twelve-measure finds `pre_dogbr` passes. So the test DOES catch a pre-set in the DOG branch.

The "branches" are sequential, not a full 2x2. But for pre-setting:

So the question is whether the derivation's claim "DOG test (set, clear) x leftover test (nonzero, zero)" actually requires all 4 cells. Let me think about this:

"DOG test" implies pre-set keyed on whether DOG is set. To catch this:
- Need at least one access with DOG set (e.g., 0x0201)
- Need at least one access with DOG clear (e.g., 0x0001 or 0)

"leftover test" implies pre-set keyed on leftover. To catch this:
- Need at least one access with leftover nonzero (e.g., 0x0201, 0x0001)
- Need at least one access with leftover zero (e.g., 0)

The pre-set has 0x0201 (DOG set, leftover nonzero), 0x0001 (DOG clear, leftover nonzero), 0 (DOG clear, leftover zero). So both DOG set and clear are covered, and both leftover nonzero and zero are covered (just not in the same 2x2 cells).

If a pre-set is keyed on (DOG set AND leftover zero) - this is a JOINT predicate. Such a predicate is in the disclosed debt: "a latch keyed on a JOINT predicate". So not catching it is disclosed. ✓

If a pre-set is keyed on "DOG set" alone (regardless of leftover) - covered by 0x0201.
If a pre-set is keyed on "leftover zero" alone - covered by 0.
If a pre-set is keyed on "leftover nonzero" alone - covered by 0x0201 or 0x0001.

So the missing 4th cell (DOG set, leftover zero) doesn't matter because:
- Pre-sets keyed on DOG set alone are covered by 0x0201
- Pre-sets keyed on DOG clear are covered by 0x0001 or 0
- Pre-sets keyed on leftover alone are covered by 0 or 0x0201

The only thing not covered is JOINT predicates, which are disclosed debt.

Actually, hmm. The pre-set has 0x0201 which has DOG set AND leftover nonzero. To detect "pre-set if DOG set", you need a DOG-set access to fire and a DOG-clear access to NOT fire. Both 0x0201 (DOG set) and 0x0001 (DOG clear) are in the pre-set, so:

Hmm wait, I need to re-read the latch behavior. The fix is:
```c
if (!(d->warned & CRIME_W_LEN)) {
    d->warned |= CRIME_W_LEN;
    fatal("...-- declined. (once) ]\n", ...);
}
goto crime_declined;
```

So if the latch is NOT set (first refusal), the announce fires and the latch is set. If the latch IS set, the announce is suppressed.

A mutation that adds `|| relative_addr == CRIME_REV` makes the check pass for REV regardless of latch state:
```c
if (!(d->warned & CRIME_W_LEN) || relative_addr == CRIME_REV) {
    d->warned |= CRIME_W_LEN;
    fatal(...);
}
```

So at REV, the notice ALWAYS prints. At other offsets, the notice prints only on first refusal.

For the test to catch this:
- Some session needs to have a refusal at a non-REV offset AFTER the latch is set somewhere (so the count would be < expected if the latch test for non-REV labels works correctly, but the count for REV bypasses).

Wait, the bypass means REV's refusal always prints. So in a session with first refusal at REV, count = 1 (correct). In a session with first refusal at non-REV, then REV after, the count would be: non-REV refusal prints (1), REV refusal prints (2), then more refusals don't print. Count = 2 (wrong, should be 1).

So for the test to catch `|| == CRIME_REV`, we need a session where REV is refused AFTER another refusal. In latch x5, REV is refused AFTER the lh+0x06 refusal. So:
- Base: lh+0x06 refuses (notice prints, count=1), lb+0x07 refuses (notice suppressed, count=1), ..., lb+0x00 refuses (notice suppressed, count=1). Count = 1 ✓
- Mutated `|| == CRIME_REV`: lh+0x06 refuses (notice prints because latch not set, count=1), lb+0x07 refuses (notice suppressed, count=1), ..., lb+0x00 refuses (notice prints because bypass, count=2). Count = 2 ✗

So the latch x5 tail includes lb+0x00 to catch `|| == CRIME_REV`. ✓
And lb+0x38 to catch `|| == CRIME_TIME`. ✓

But what about `|| == CRIME_CONTROL`? A bypass at CONTROL. Is CONTROL refused in latch x5 after the latch is set?

Looking at the latch x5 refusals:
- lh +0x06
- lb +0x07
- lbu +0x3f
- sb +0x37
- sh +0x36
- lb +0x00 (REV)
- lb +0x38 (TIME)

None of these are at +0x08 (CONTROL). So a mutation `|| == CRIME_CONTROL` would not be caught by latch x5.

But the brief says: "the latch TEST that lets one label through (`|| relative_addr == L'): every label is refused again AFTER the latch is set somewhere -- REV and TIME in latch x5's tail". So only REV and TIME are explicitly named as caught. Other labels (CONTROL, INTSTAT, INTMASK, DOG) - are they caught elsewhere?

Looking at other sessions:
- rev once (lb +0x00 then lb +0x07): first refusal at REV. With `|| == CRIME_CONTROL`, no effect (REV is refused, prints notice, +0x07 refuses, suppressed). Count = 1. Same as base. So this doesn't catch it.
- sh08b once (sb/sh at +0x08, then lb at +0x07): first refusal at CONTROL. With `|| == CRIME_CONTROL`, bypass at CONTROL, notice prints at +0x08, then +0x07 refused, suppressed. Count = 1. Same as base. So this doesn't catch it (it would EXPECT 1 but get 1).
  - Wait, with the mutation, the notice ALWAYS prints at CONTROL regardless of latch. So the first refusal at CONTROL prints (count=1). Then +0x07 is refused, latch is set (or already set), suppressed (count=1). Same as base.
  - Hmm, so `|| == CRIME_CONTROL` isn't caught by sh08b once.

Actually let me think more carefully. The bypass makes the notice print at L regardless of latch state. So:
- Session where L is the FIRST refusal: notice prints (count=1). Then later refusals at non-L are suppressed (count stays 1). Same as base.
- Session where L is NOT the first refusal: first refusal sets latch and prints (count=1). Later refusals at non-L are suppressed (count stays 1). When L is refused, bypass makes notice print again (count=2). DIFFERENT from base.

So `|| == L` is caught only if L is refused AFTER another refusal in some session. In latch x5, the labels refused after another refusal are REV (+0x00) and TIME (+0x38). Other labels (CONTROL, INTSTAT, INTMASK, DOG) are not refused after the latch is set in latch x5.

Hmm, but in other sessions:

Hmm, so `|| == CRIME_INTMASK` would not be caught by imask once. Let me see if there's any session that has INTMASK refused after another refusal.

Looking at the pre-set prologue of latch x5: there's `("lw", 0x18)` which is an ACCEPTED access at INTMASK, not a refusal. So no refused INTMASK in latch x5 tail.

What about other sessions? The pre-set list itself doesn't refuse INTMASK. The other sessions that refuse INTMASK (imask once, imask lb, etc.) have INTMASK as the FIRST refusal.

So a mutation `|| == CRIME_INTMASK` might not be caught!

Wait, let me check if this is in the disclosed debt. Looking at the DISCLOSED section:

So a latch keyed on ONE NAMED OFFSET is in the floor if that offset is in the generating set. The generating set is: REV, TIME (from latch x5 tail), and the default-arm cells.

If INTMASK is refused only at the START of a session (before the latch can be set), then `|| == CRIME_INTMASK` would always print notice at INTMASK. This means:
- INTMASK session: prints notice (count=1), then +0x07 refused, suppressed (count=1). Base also gives 1. Not caught.
- Default-arm sessions: first refusal sets latch, prints notice. INTMASK access in such a session... but is INTMASK accessed in any default-arm session?

```python

    ...
for op, off, val in (("sb", 0x37, 0xab), ("sh", 0x36, 0xabcd)):
    x, _ = st(A, op, CRIME + off, val); c += x
for off in (0x00, 0x38):
    x, _ = ld(A, "lb", CRIME + off, "v0"); c += x
```

So the refusals are at: +0x06 (default), +0x07 (default), +0x3f (default), +0x37 (default), +0x36 (default), +0x00 (REV), +0x38 (TIME). INTMASK (+0x18) is NOT in the refusals.

- If NO pre-set mutation (just byp_rev): latch is NOT set before first refusal. lh+0x06: latch not set, condition true, announce fires, count=1. ### Question 2: Floor gap

Is there a floor gap in the 131-row test? An edit that undoes part of the fix's behavior and still scores 131/131.

Let me think about possible gaps:

1. **Accept set shrunk at an unrowed slot**: disclosed. An accept arm narrowed by an address strictly inside the window at an unrowed slot is not seen.

2. **A refused store that lands only at +0x20 (SOFTINT)**: this is the `c3_soft_land` mutant, which is in the disclosed list. It's predicted SURVIVOR and 131/131. So this is known.

3. **Bypass mutations at CONTROL, INTSTAT, INTMASK, DOG**: not tested. A bypass at these labels would not be caught. But this isn't an "undo" of the fix per se - it's an added behavior.

4. **Effect keyed on ONE UNNAMED offset**: disclosed.

5. **A function-static latch**: disclosed (can't see it from one process).

6. **Widths 3, 5, 6, 7**: disclosed.

7. **Re-arm or pre-set inside HARD_RESET branch**: disclosed (stops CPUs).

8. **Refused sb/sh that LANDS only at CRIME_TIME**: OPEN AND SAID SO. "ONE NAMED CELL IS OPEN AND SAID SO (kimi-k3:cloud, closing pass): a refused sb/sh that LANDS only at CRIME_TIME stores <= 0xffff, which the time rows' 0.1 s threshold cannot tell from the 66-990 the first tick adds -- clause (e) debt."

So clause (e) debt is explicitly disclosed.

Hmm, this is a comment in the diff. It says "codex, third closing pass" found that a refused store that CLEARS (e.g., `&= idata`) would leave the unseeded slot at 0. So hi keeps wanted a non-zero value to verify the refused store didn't land. So they seed it first.

But wait, what's the behavior of a refused store? The decline path goes through `goto crime_declined;` which skips the register switch. So a refused store DOES NOT update the register at all (not even with &= or |=). The register keeps whatever value it had.

Hmm, so "a refused store that CLEARS" - this is hypothetical. The current code doesn't do this. But if a mutation made the decline path NOT goto (e.g., fell through), then the register switch would be reached, and the refused store's effect would be... well, depends on the switch arm.

Wait, actually, looking at the function: in the current fix, the decline goes to `goto crime_declined`, skipping the register switch. The register is NOT updated for declined accesses.

If a mutation removes the `goto crime_declined;`, the declined access falls through to the register switch. The switch arms for INTSTAT, INTMASK, DOG, TIME, etc. don't update the register (they just call functions or break). So even without the goto, a declined access to these labels wouldn't update the register.

But for REV (writes: nothing happens in the body for writes) and CONTROL (writes: the if (writeflag == MEM_WRITE) body could mutate the register).

Hmm wait, the register is read from `idata` into the function, but it's stored via `d->reg[relative_addr / 8] = idata` ONLY in the len==8 and len==4 paths. The decline path skips these too. So the register is not modified.

But the switch arms don't modify the register either. So even without the goto, the register would not change for declined accesses.

Unless the fall-through is only partial (e.g., removed goto but kept the rest). Then the declined access would... still not modify the register, since the len-based update is before the switch.

Wait, the goto is AFTER the if/else if/else for len. So the structure is:
```
if (len == 8) { ... } 
else if (len == 4) { ... }
else { goto crime_declined; }
switch (relative_addr) { ... }
crime_declined: ...
```

If a mutation removed the `goto crime_declined;` (so just `}` after the else), then the decline path falls through to the switch. The switch could modify behavior (e.g., CRIME_CONTROL arm mutates the register via idata, but for a declined access, idata is what was read).

Wait, in the decline path:
- if (writeflag == MEM_WRITE) idata = memory_readmax64(cpu, data, len);
- Otherwise idata stays 0.

For a declined access (len not 4 or 8), the data is the bytes the guest tried to write. idata is the assembled value.

For example, sb 0x40: data = [0x40], idata = 0x40 (since len=1, memory_readmax64 returns the byte in low bits).

If this falls through to the switch and hits CRIME_CONTROL (relative_addr = 0x08), the if (writeflag == MEM_WRITE) body would execute:
- idata & DOG_ENABLE: if idata = 0x40, no DOG. No action.
- idata & HARD_RESET: if idata = 0x40, no HARD_RESET. No action.
- idata &= ~ENDIANESS: 0x40 & ~0x100 = 0x40.
- if (idata != 0) fatal(): 0x40 != 0, so fatal "[ CRIME_CONTROL: unimplemented control 0x0000000000000040 ]".

So a declined sb 0x40 at CONTROL would print the CONTROL fatal if the goto is removed. That's caught by `sh08b msg` (sh 0x0800 at CONTROL prints HARD_RESET-related... actually, that's about HARD_RESET, not the unimplemented control). Hmm.

Actually, `sh08b msg` checks for the "CRIME_CONTROL: unimplemented" message. So removing the goto for a refused store at CONTROL would print this message. Caught.

- INTSTAT/INTMASK: the switch arm calls crime_interrupt_reassert(d). This doesn't modify the register. No side effect on register. But the call might have side effects (e.g., raising IP2). Hmm, this doesn't change behavior (latch is always set, fatal only fires if not set before, which is now never since we just set it).

```c
odata = d->reg[relative_addr / 8];
if (relative_addr & 4)
    odata = (int32_t)odata;
else
    odata = (int32_t)(odata >> 32);
```

So if `relative_addr & 4` (high half): odata = (int32_t)odata (lower 32 bits).
Else (low half): odata = (int32_t)(odata >> 32) (upper 32 bits).

For 4-byte read at +0x08 (low half): odata = (int32_t)(reg[1] >> 32) = (int32_t)(0x0000000000000100 >> 32) = 0. So a 4-byte read at CONTROL answers 0.

For 4-byte read at +0x0c (high half): odata = (int32_t)(reg[1]) = (int32_t)(0x0000000000000100) = 0x100.

For 8-byte read at +0x08: odata = reg[1] = 0x100.

OK so CONTROL has different answers at different widths. Now, if a mutation makes the decline path return the register value at the requested width:

For 4-byte read at +0x08: odata = (int32_t)(reg[1] >> 32) = 0. Same as decline's 0. NOT CAUGHT.
For 8-byte read at +0x08: odata = reg[1] = 0x100. Not 0. Caught.
For 4-byte read at +0x0c: odata = (int32_t)(reg[1]) = 0x100. Not 0. Caught.

Hmm, so a mutation that returns the register's high half at +0x08 (4-byte) would not be caught (because the high half is 0).

But this is the disclosed "byte selection that the model holds at 0" disclosure. The model's high half (upper 32 bits) of CONTROL is 0 (since CONTROL = 0x100, upper 32 = 0). So this is EQUIVALENT.

But wait, the test should distinguish 4-byte reads at +0x08 from 4-byte reads at +0x0c. Let me check.

Looking at the diff, I don't see explicit 4-byte reads at +0x0c. The test does have `lw +0x04` and `lw +0x08` (4-byte reads). Wait, where's `lw +0x0c`?

Hmm, I don't see lw +0x0c in the test. So 4-byte reads at +0x0c might not be tested.

But the brief mentions "NetBSD's +0x0c write" in the crimectllane disclosure. So +0x0c writes are a thing. But the test might not have 4-byte reads at +0x0c.

If a mutation makes the decline path return the register value (at the requested width), then:
- 4-byte read at +0x08 (low half): returns upper 32 bits = 0. Not caught (decline also 0).
- 4-byte read at +0x0c (high half): returns lower 32 bits = 0x100. Caught (if tested).
- 8-byte read at +0x08: returns full 8 bytes = 0x100. Caught.

But if the test doesn't have a 4-byte read at +0x0c, then the high-half answer at +0x0c is not tested. And the mutation would not be caught at +0x0c.

But wait, the 4-byte answer at +0x08 (low half) returns 0 (EQUIVALENT to decline). So a mutation that returns the register's HIGH half at +0x08 (4-byte) is EQUIVALENT. No bug.

A mutation that returns the register's LOW half at +0x08 (4-byte) would return 0x100 (since CONTROL = 0x100, lower 32 = 0x100). But wait, the 4-byte path at +0x08 returns `(int32_t)(reg[1] >> 32) = 0`. If a mutation changes the decline to return `reg[1] & 0xffffffff` (low half), that's 0x100. Different from decline's 0.

Hmm, but this is the 4-byte path's NORMAL behavior - the 4-byte read at +0x08 returns the low half in some interpretation. Let me re-check.

Actually, the 4-byte path:
- `odata = d->reg[relative_addr / 8];` (read full 8 bytes)
- if (relative_addr & 4) (high half): odata = (int32_t)odata (lower 32 bits)
- else (low half): odata = (int32_t)(odata >> 32) (upper 32 bits)

For CONTROL = 0x100 stored in reg[1]:
- 4-byte read at +0x08 (low half, since 0x08 & 4 = 0): odata = (int32_t)(0x100 >> 32) = 0
- 4-byte read at +0x0c (high half, since 0x0c & 4 = 4): odata = (int32_t)(0x100) = 0x100

So 4-byte read at +0x0c returns 0x100, which is CONTROL's value. And 4-byte read at +0x08 returns 0.

Now, the decline path: odata is initialized to 0. For declined reads, odata stays 0. Answer is 0.

A mutation `odata = d->reg[CRIME_CONTROL / 8];` (return full 8 bytes) for refused reads at CONTROL:
- 8-byte: returns 0x100. Caught.
- 4-byte: depends on the path. If the mutation just sets odata = reg, then for 4-byte reads, the tail's memory_writemax64(cpu, data, len, odata) writes odata (0x100) to data (only len bytes). So data[0..3] = 0x100 (little-endian). Answer is 0x100. Caught.

Wait, but if the mutation is in the decline path BEFORE the tail, then odata = 0x100 for refused reads. The tail writes the low len bytes to data. So for 4-byte read, data = [0x00, 0x01, 0x00, 0x00] (little-endian 0x100). Answer is 0x100. Caught.

Hmm, so a mutation that makes the decline path return the full register value is caught at CONTROL.

What about a mutation that returns just one byte? `odata = d->reg[CRIME_CONTROL / 8] & 0xff;`? For CONTROL = 0x100, & 0xff = 0. Same as decline. EQUIVALENT. Not caught. But disclosed.

OK so this is in the disclosed debt.

But INTSTAT is a labeled case. If a mutation makes the decline path WRITE to INTSTAT (e.g., `d->reg[CRIME_INTSTAT / sizeof(uint64_t)] = idata;`), then:
- The register is updated. The next accepted read at INTSTAT shows the new value.
- The tail recomputes INTSTAT (= SOFTINT | HARDINT). So INTSTAT is overwritten by the recompute. The mutation's write is lost.

Wait, looking at the function: the tail recomputes INTSTAT. So even if the decline path writes to INTSTAT, the tail would overwrite it. The mutation has no net effect.

Actually wait, the tail recomputes INTSTAT (which uses SOFTINT and HARDINT). If a refused store at INTMASK writes to INTMASK, the tail doesn't recompute INTMASK. So the write persists. `imask keeps` catches this.

But for INTSTAT, the tail recomputes it. So a refused store at INTSTAT that writes to INTSTAT is overwritten by the tail. Net effect: no change to INTSTAT. So `istat keeps` would not catch it (because INTSTAT is unchanged either way).

But wait, what about a pre-set keyed on "len == 4 && relative_addr & 4" (HIGH half only)? This is a JOINT predicate, disclosed.

What about a pre-set keyed on "relative_addr & 4" (just the half, regardless of len)? Then 4-byte accesses with HIGH half would fire, but 8-byte accesses (no half branch) wouldn't. If a pre-set is added to the HIGH half branch:
```c
if (relative_addr & 4) {
    d->reg[relative_addr / 8] &= ~0xffffffffULL;
    d->reg[relative_addr / 8] |= (uint32_t)idata;
    d->warned |= CRIME_W_LEN; // pre-set here
}
```

The pre-set fires for 4-byte accesses with HIGH half. 8-byte accesses don't reach this code.

To catch this, we need a 4-byte access with HIGH half in the pre-set. ✓ (sw at +0x04, sw at +0x34).
And we need a 4-byte access with LOW half (where the pre-set doesn't fire). ✓ (sw at +0x08, sw at +0x30).

If the pre-set is keyed on "relative_addr & 4" only, then HIGH half fires, LOW half doesn't. Both kinds of accesses are in the pre-set, so the latch is set for HIGH half accesses and not for LOW half accesses. The first refusal after a HIGH half access sees the latch set; after a LOW half access, doesn't see it.

So this is caught by the pre-set having both HIGH and LOW half accesses.

But wait, the order matters. In the pre-set prologue:
- sw +0x08 (LOW half, CONTROL)
- sw +0x34 (HIGH half, default)
- lw +0x34 (HIGH half, default)
- ...

If sw +0x08 (LOW half) is first, latch not set. Then sw +0x34 (HIGH half, pre-set fires, latch set). Then lw +0x34 (HIGH half)... no, the pre-set is in the WRITE half, not the read half. So lw +0x34 doesn't trigger the pre-set (mutation only added to write side).

So after the pre-set prologue, latch is set. First refusal sees latch, suppressed. Count = 0. Row expects 1. Caught.

OK so the pre-set catches "relative_addr & 4" pre-set mutations.

### Question 2: Floor gap

Let me think about a SPECIFIC mutation that escapes. The question wants an exact edit.

Hmm. Let me think about what "undoes part of the fix" means.

One specific gap: "a refused sb/sh that LANDS only at CRIME_TIME stores <= 0xffff, which the time rows' 0.1 s threshold cannot tell from the 66-990 the first tick adds -- clause (e) debt."

This is disclosed. The mutation:
```c
if (writeflag == MEM_WRITE && relative_addr == CRIME_TIME)
    d->reg[CRIME_TIME / sizeof(uint64_t)] = idata;
```
added to the decline path.

This mutation makes refused sb/sh at TIME land (store <= 0xffff). The time rows check the answer (which would still be 0 because odata is initialized to 0). The mutation writes to the register, but odata is still 0 for the read. So the test doesn't catch this.

Actually wait, let me re-read. The brief says "a refused sb/sh that LANDS only at CRIME_TIME". So this is a refused STORE that lands (writes to the register). The mutation writes idata to the register.

For the test to detect this, it would need to read back the register after the refused store and check the value. The test has `time keeps` - wait, I don't see this row.

Let me look at the diff for any keeps row at TIME... no, I don't see one.

So a mutation that makes refused sb/sh at TIME land would not be caught by the test. Disclosed.

This is the only "open" floor gap I can identify.

Are there others?

Actually, let me re-read the brief for any other open cells:

"ONE NAMED CELL IS OPEN AND SAID SO (kimi-k3:cloud, closing pass): a refused sb/sh that LANDS only at CRIME_TIME stores <= 0xffff"

This is the ONE open cell. Other named cells are caught.

But the brief also says: "An effect keyed on a single ADDRESS BIT is NOT in this class: every bit 0-9 is carried both ways by a refused access of each kind that can carry it (the mid session sets bits 0-8, the far block bit 9; an aligned 2-byte access never sets bit 0; kimi-k3:cloud, second closing pass, found bits 7 and 8 carried by no refusal at all)."

So address bits 7 and 8 are NOT carried by any refusal at all (in earlier tests). The new test has mid once now refusing at +0x07 as well (per the diff). Let me check.

The diff says:
"and a follow-up refusal with bits 7 and 8 CLEAR (codex, third closing pass: a latch whose bit is keyed on address bit 7 or 8 was invisible -- the middle block's refusals all set them, every other session's all clear them) x, _ = ld(A, "lb", CRIME + 0x07, "s3"); c += x"

So mid once now has a lb +0x07 refusal. +0x07 has bits 7 and 8 clear (since 0x07 = 0b111, bit 7 is 0, bit 8 is 0). The mid session's refusals (+0x1ff, +0x1fe, +0x1fc) all have bits 7 and 8 set. So lb +0x07 has bits 7 and 8 clear.

A latch keyed on bit 7 would fire for the mid session's refusals (bit 7 set) but not for the lb +0x07 follow-up (bit 7 clear). Caught.
A latch keyed on bit 8 similarly.

OK so address bits 7 and 8 are now covered.

But what about bit 0? "an aligned 2-byte access never sets bit 0". So a 2-byte (lh, sh) access has bit 0 clear (since offset is even). Bit 0 is only set by 1-byte (lb, sb) accesses at odd offsets.

For an effect keyed on bit 0: 1-byte at odd offsets (e.g., lb +0x07) has bit 0 set. 1-byte at even offsets (e.g., lb +0x06) has bit 0 clear. 2-byte accesses (lh, sh) have bit 0 clear.

So bit 0 is set by some refusals (lb +0x07) and clear by others (lb +0x06, lh +0x06, sb +0x36, etc.). Caught.

OK so all bits 0-9 are covered.

6. Let me check the lo keeps row:

```python
x, _ = st(A, "sd", CRIME + 0x40, 0x0123456789abcdef); c += x
x, _ = st(A, "sb", CRIME + 0x47, 0xff); c += x
x, _ = st(A, "sh", CRIME + 0x44, 0xffff); c += x
x, _ = ld(A, "ld", CRIME + 0x40, "s2"); c += x
t, clean = session(c + ["print a0", "print a1", "print v0", "print s2"])
row("lo keeps", pr(t, "s2"), "0x123456789abcdef", "refused sb +0x47, sh +0x44 (bit 4 clear) leave +0x40 seeded")
```

So lo keeps seeds +0x40 with 0x0123456789abcdef, then refuses sb at +0x47 (bit 4 clear: 0x47 = 0b1000111, bit 4 = 0) and sh at +0x44 (bit 4 clear: 0x44 = 0b1000100, bit 4 = 0).

If a refused store at +0x47 or +0x44 LANDS, the register at +0x40 would be modified. The read back at +0x40 (ld) shows the modified value.

For +0x47 (offset 7 within register 8 starting at +0x40): sb writes to byte at +0x47 (the high byte of the register). If lands, the high byte of +0x40 register is set to 0xff. Read back: high byte 0xff, low bytes unchanged. Value = 0xff23456789abcdef. Not 0x0123456789abcdef. Caught.

For +0x44 (offset 4 within register 8 starting at +0x40): sh writes to halfword at +0x44 (the high halfword of the register). If lands, the high halfword is set to 0xffff. Read back: high halfword 0xffff, low halfword unchanged. Value = 0xffff23456789abcdef. Wait, that's 9 bytes. Let me re-think.

Actually, the register is 8 bytes. +0x40 to +0x47. The halfword at +0x44 is bytes at +0x44 and +0x45. Setting them to 0xffff means high halfword of low word = 0xffff. So the value becomes 0xffff23456789abcdef - wait, this is 9 hex digits.

Let me re-think. The 8-byte value at +0x40 register is `XX XX XX XX XX XX XX XX` (8 bytes, MSB first). The halfword at +0x44 is bytes 4 and 5 (0-indexed). Setting them to 0xff 0xff means bytes 4 and 5 are 0xff. So the value is `XX XX XX XX ff ff XX XX` (where XX is original).

Original: 0x0123456789abcdef. Bytes 0-7 (MSB first): 01 23 45 67 89 ab cd ef.
Bytes 4-5: 89 ab. Setting to ff ff: 01 23 45 67 ff ff cd ef = 0x01234567ffffcdef. Hmm, that's not right.

Wait, let me think about endianness. The 8-byte value 0x0123456789abcdef in memory (big-endian) is 01 23 45 67 89 ab cd ef. In little-endian, it's ef cd ab 89 67 45 23 01.

The halfword at +0x44 (offset 4): in memory, this is bytes at offset 4 and 5. In big-endian, these are bytes 4 and 5 of the value. In little-endian, these are bytes 4 and 5 of the value (just read in reverse).

Actually, MIPS is typically big-endian or little-endian depending on the bit. For simplicity, let me assume the byte at +0x40 is the MSB and +0x47 is the LSB.

Original value 0x0123456789abcdef in memory (MSB at +0x40):
+0x40: 01
+0x41: 23
+0x42: 45
+0x43: 67
+0x44: 89
+0x45: ab
+0x46: cd
+0x47: ef

sb at +0x47 with 0xff: sets byte at +0x47 to 0xff. New value: 0x0123456789abcdff.
sh at +0x44 with 0xffff: sets halfword at +0x44 (bytes 4-5) to 0xff 0xff. New value: 0x01234567ffffcdef.

Wait, sh stores a halfword (2 bytes). At offset +0x44, the halfword is bytes 4 and 5 (MSB at +0x44, LSB at +0x45). Setting them to 0xffff means +0x44 = 0xff, +0x45 = 0xff. New value: 0x012345 67 ff ff cd ef = 0x01234567ffffcdef. Hmm, that's 9 hex digits, but it's actually 0x01234567ffffcdef which is 16 hex digits (8 bytes). Let me recount.

Original: 01 23 45 67 89 ab cd ef (8 bytes)
After sh at +0x44 with 0xffff: bytes 4-5 changed from 89 ab to ff ff.
New: 01 23 45 67 ff ff cd ef = 0x01234567ffffcdef.

So the read back at +0x40 (ld) would show 0x01234567ffffcdef, not 0x0123456789abcdef. Caught by lo keeps.

OK so lo keeps works.

Now, the brief says "every read-back refused store had bit 4 set" (Opus seat measured). So earlier refused stores all had bit 4 set. The new lo keeps adds a refused store at +0x47 (bit 4 clear, since 0x47 = 0b1000111, bit 4 = 0) and sh at +0x44 (bit 4 clear, 0x44 = 0b1000100, bit 4 = 0).

So the lo keeps row catches a refused store that lands only at offsets with bit 4 clear. Without this row, such a mutation would pass.

But wait, the brief says "every read-back refused store had bit 4 set". This means earlier read-back stores were at offsets with bit 4 set. So a mutation keyed on "bit 4 clear" would pass earlier tests. The new lo keeps catches it.

Hmm, so lo keeps is a meaningful addition.

7. Let me check the time sd lb v0 row:

```python
w2, _ = ld(A, "lb", CRIME + 0x08, "a1")
ts, _ = st(A, "sd", CRIME + 0x38, 0x11111111)
tb, _ = ld(A, "lb", CRIME + 0x38, "s0")

```

So TIME is seeded with 0x11111111 (sd +0x38 with 0x11111111). Then refused lb at +0x38 should answer 0 (decline), not 0x11.

If a mutation makes the decline path answer the register at TIME, the refused lb would answer 0x11 (low byte). Not 0. Caught.

The seed 0x11111111 has low byte 0x11 (odd, nonzero). Low halfword 0x1111 (nonzero). So any byte selection of the seed is nonzero. Caught.

But what about an EVEN seed? If TIME were seeded with an even value (e.g., 0x10000000), the low byte is 0x00. A mutation answering the low byte would return 0x00, same as decline. Not caught.

So the seed must be ODD (nonzero low byte) and the low halfword must be nonzero. 0x11111111 satisfies this.

But the brief says "TIME: at the time rows' refused reads TIME is a multiple of 66 and is 0 in 42 of 800 logged cells (the same seat -- this said "0.3% of runs, 66-990 includes 256, 512, 768", and those are not multiples of 66)".

Wait, 256, 512, 768 are not multiples of 66? Let me check: 256 / 66 = 3.879, not integer. 512 / 66 = 7.758, not integer. 768 / 66 = 11.636, not integer. So they're not multiples of 66.

But the brief says TIME is "a multiple of 66". If TIME is the result of tick updates, each tick adds 66. So TIME is a multiple of 66 from the start. But 0 is a multiple of 66 (0 = 0 * 66). So TIME could be 0 in some runs.

Actually, the brief says TIME is "a multiple of 66" and "0 in 42 of 800 logged cells". So in 42/800 = 5.25% of runs, TIME is 0. The rest, TIME is 66, 132, 198, ..., 990.

A multiple of 66 that's 0 is just 0. Other multiples of 66 (66, 132, ...) are nonzero.

So a refused read at TIME answers either 0 (decline) or the TIME value. If TIME is 0 in some runs, the answer is 0 either way. Not distinguishable.

If TIME is nonzero (most runs), the answer is 0 (decline) vs TIME value (mutation). Distinguishable.

But the test should work in all runs, not just most. So seeding TIME with an odd value ensures TIME is always nonzero. Good.

8. Let me check the KINDS list (re-arm) vs pre-set list:

KINDS:
```python
KINDS = (("sd", 0x30, 0), ("ld", 0x30, 0), ("sw", 0x34, 0), ("lw", 0x34, 0), ("sw", 0x30, 0), ("lw", 0x30, 0),
         ("ld", 0x38, 0), ("lw", 0x18, 0), ("lw", 0x10, 0), ("lw", 0x00, 0), ("lwP", 0x00, 0),
         ("sw", 0x08, 0x0201), ("sw", 0x08, 0x0001), ("sw", 0x08, 0), ("lw", 0x08, 0),
         ("lw", 0x04, 0), ("sw", 0x04, 0), ("sd", 0x278, 0), ("ld", 0x278, 0))
```

Pre-set:
```python
for op, off, val in (("sw", 0x08, 0x0201), ("sw", 0x08, 0x0001), ("sw", 0x08, 0), ("sw", 0x34, 0x0badcafe)):
    x, _ = st(A, op, CRIME + off, val); c += x
x, _ = ld(A, "lw", CRIME + 0x34, "a0"); c += x
x, _ = ld(A, "lw", CRIME + 0x08, "a0"); c += x
x, _ = ld(A, "lw", CRIME + 0x00, "a1", at=PROBE_PC); c += x
x, _ = ld(A, "lw", CRIME + 0x00, "a1"); c += x
for op, off in (("lw", 0x10), ("lw", 0x18), ("ld", 0x30), ("ld", 0x38)):
    x, _ = ld(A, op, CRIME + off, "a2"); c += x
x, _ = st(A, "sd", CRIME + 0x278, 0); c += x
```

KINDS entries:
- sd +0x30 (8W at DOG): NOT in pre-set
- ld +0x30 (8R at DOG): in pre-set
- sw +0x34 (4W high at default): in pre-set (with val 0x0badcafe)
- lw +0x34 (4R high at default): in pre-set
- sw +0x30 (4W low at DOG): NOT in pre-set
- lw +0x30 (4R low at DOG): NOT in pre-set
- ld +0x38 (8R at TIME): in pre-set
- lw +0x18 (4R low at INTMASK): in pre-set
- lw +0x10 (4R low at INTSTAT): in pre-set
- lw +0x00 (4R low at REV): in pre-set
- lwP +0x00 (4R low at REV from PROM): in pre-set
- sw +0x08 with 3 values: in pre-set
- lw +0x08 (4R low at CONTROL): in pre-set
- lw +0x04 (4R high at default): NOT in pre-set
- sw +0x04 (4W high at default): NOT in pre-set
- sd +0x278 (8W at default far): in pre-set (with val 0)
- ld +0x278 (8R at default far): NOT in pre-set

Offsets: 0x00, 0x08, 0x10, 0x18, 0x30, 0x38.

In binary:
- 0x00 = 000000
- 0x08 = 001000
- 0x10 = 010000
- 0x18 = 011000
- 0x30 = 110000
- 0x38 = 111000

Common pattern: bits 3, 4, 5 in various combinations. No bit 1 set.

- 0x10 = 00010000, bit 1 = 0
- 0x18 = 00011000, bit 1 = 0
- 0x30 = 00110000, bit 1 = 0
- 0x38 = 00111000, bit 1 = 0

All bit 1 = 0. So claim is false.

Hmm, this is interesting. The disclosure says `goto crime_declined` is equivalent to `return 1` on every channel. But `return 1` would return from the function, skipping the tail (which restores HARDINT and recomputes INTSTAT).

Wait, "on every channel" might mean "for every test channel" (every row in the test). The brief says this is measured 40/40 (on the then 40-row test).

Hmm, but if `return 1` skips the tail, then INTSTAT is not recomputed. For declined accesses, INTSTAT was set in the prelude. The recompute in the tail would set INTSTAT = SOFTINT | HARDINT (using the current HARDINT). If HARDINT is unchanged, the recompute is identity.

But there's `d->reg[CRIME_HARDINT / sizeof(uint64_t)] = preserved_CRIME_HARDINT;` which restores HARDINT. If HARDINT was modified during the function (which it's not), the restore would matter. As-is, it's identity.

So skipping the tail is OK for declined accesses (since HARDINT is preserved, INTSTAT recompute is identity).

For accepted accesses, the tail is reached. If we change to `return 1`, accepted accesses would also skip the tail. But the tail is needed for accepted accesses? Let me check.

Looking at the function: the tail is reached for all accesses (accepted and declined). For accepted accesses, the tail restores HARDINT and recomputes INTSTAT. As noted, these are identities.

The tail also does `memory_writemax64(cpu, data, len, odata);` for reads. For accepted reads, odata is set by the len==8 or len==4 path. The tail writes odata to data.

If we skip the tail (`return 1`), then accepted reads wouldn't have their data written. The data buffer would be unchanged. But memory_readmax64 was called for the IDATA path (writes), not for ODATA.

Wait, looking at the function:
- For writes: idata = memory_readmax64(cpu, data, len) at the start. The function uses idata.
- For reads: odata is set in the len==8 or len==4 path. The tail writes odata via memory_writemax64.

So for reads, odata -> data happens in the tail. For writes, data -> idata happens at the start.

If we skip the tail (`return 1` instead of `goto crime_declined`):
- For reads: odata is set but never written to data. The data buffer is whatever was passed in (which is the buffer before this call). The caller would see no change.

But this would BREAK accepted reads! The test would catch this (since accepted reads expect specific values).

Hmm, but the brief says `goto crime_declined' -> `return 1' is EQUIVALENT on every channel`. Maybe "every channel" excludes accepted read channels? Or maybe `return 1` here only applies to declined accesses?

Looking at the brief: "Measured 40/40 (the then 40-row test) in kill table 1."

So this was measured on the 40-row test. The 40-row test was an earlier version. Maybe at that time, the test didn't have rows that would catch this.

Or maybe the disclosure is saying: in the current test, `goto crime_declined' -> `return 1' is EQUIVALENT on every channel [in the 40-row test]`. The 40-row test might not have had accepted-read channels.

Hmm, this is confusing. Let me not worry about this for now.

26. Let me think about what other "small source edits" could escape.


But this is essentially adding a side effect, which is in the disclosed class (effect keyed on... something).

```c

goto crime_declined;
```

Then for declined accesses with idata = 0xdeadbeef, the first goto fires (skip side effect). For other declined accesses, the side effect fires, then goto.

But the side effect would be observable. The test might catch it (depending on what it is).

Hmm.

Actually, let me think about a specific mutation:

```c
}
```

Then for declined accesses (len != 4 && len != 8), nothing happens, falls through to switch. This is the fall-through class. Caught by item 3.

**Edit YY**: Make the announce block conditional on something not in the test:

But `some_flag` would need to be set somewhere. If it's never set, no announce. Caught by count rows.

**Edit XX**: Make the goto conditional on something:


If `some_condition` is always false, fall through. Caught.

If `some_condition` is keyed on something the test doesn't vary, escapes. But the test varies many things (direction, width, offset, etc.). Hard to find a condition that's always false in the test.

OK I don't see an obvious edit that escapes.

OK let me write up my findings.

### Summary of findings:

1. **Derivation verification**:
   - The pre-set list is "single-axis complete" for the claimed branches (len 8, len 4 halves, every case label, REV pc-window, CONTROL writeflag/DOG/leftover, default arm).
   - The pre-set has more coverage for some branches (e.g., sd +0x278 covers 8W, lw +0x34 covers 4R high at default). The re-arm (KINDS) list is slightly more comprehensive (has ld +0x278 and sw +0x30 which the pre-set lacks), but they're redundant for single-axis coverage.
   - The derivation claim "every label is refused again AFTER the latch is set somewhere" is MISLEADING: only REV and TIME are in latch x5's tail. Other labels (CONTROL, INTSTAT, INTMASK, DOG) are NOT refused after latch is set. But this is consistent with the test scope (only byp_rev, byp_time are in the table).
   - The brief's claim "every said row sat at an offset with bit 1 set" is FALSE for all said rows (bit 1 is clear for all). The contradiction is acknowledged ("rev said is at +0x00, every address bit clear") but the general claim is still wrong.

2. **Floor gap**:
   - The disclosed gap: refused sb/sh at CRIME_TIME that lands. The mutation `if (writeflag == MEM_WRITE && relative_addr == CRIME_TIME) d->reg[CRIME_TIME / 8] = idata;` in the decline path escapes the test.
   - This is disclosed as clause (e) debt.
   - Other potential gaps (e.g., bypass at CONTROL/INTSTAT/INTMASK/DOG) are also disclosed (single-axis generating set excludes these labels).

3. **Wrong statements**:
   - "every said row sat at an offset with bit 1 set" is false. (Possibly a typo or unclear phrasing.)
   - "every label is refused again AFTER the latch is set somewhere" is overstated. Only REV and TIME are in the latch x5 tail.

Let me also double-check the wrong statement about bit 1:

The brief says: "every said row sat at an offset with bit 1 set -- rev said is at +0x00, every address bit clear"

I interpret this as:
- (Claim) Every said row sat at an offset with bit 1 set
- (Exception/clarification) Rev said is at +0x00, every address bit clear

But ALL said rows are at offsets with every address bit clear in some bits. Let me re-check:
- rev said at +0x00: bit 0, 1, 2, 3, 4, ... all clear
- sh08b said at +0x08: bit 0, 1, 2 clear; bit 3 SET
- imask said at +0x18: bit 0, 1, 2 clear; bit 3, 4 SET
- istat said at +0x10: bit 0, 1, 2 clear; bit 4 SET
- dog said at +0x30: bit 0, 1, 2 clear; bit 4, 5 SET
- time said at +0x38: bit 0, 1, 2 clear; bit 3, 4, 5 SET

So:
- bit 0: clear for all
- bit 1: clear for all
- bit 2: clear for all
- bit 3: SET for sh08b, imask, time; clear for rev, istat, dog
- bit 4: SET for imask, istat, dog, time; clear for rev, sh08b

The only bit that's set for SOME said rows is bit 3 or bit 4. But the brief says "bit 1 set".

Maybe the brief is using a different bit numbering convention? Like "bit 1" = the second bit from MSB? Let me check:
- +0x00: 00000000, second from MSB (bit 6 in 0-indexed LSB) = 0
- +0x08: 00001000, second from MSB = 0
- +0x10: 00010000, second from MSB = 0
- +0x18: 00011000, second from MSB = 0
- +0x30: 00110000, second from MSB = 1
- +0x38: 00111000, second from MSB = 1

So bit 6 (or "bit 1" from MSB) is SET for +0x30, +0x38, and clear for +0x00, +0x08, +0x10, +0x18.

That's 2 out of 6, not "every". So this interpretation also doesn't fit.

Hmm. Maybe "bit 1 set" is a typo for "bit 3 set" or "bit 4 set"?

bit 3 set:
- +0x08, +0x18, +0x38 (3 rows)
- Clear for +0x00, +0x10, +0x30 (3 rows)

bit 4 set:
- +0x18, +0x10, +0x30, +0x38 (4 rows)
- Clear for +0x00, +0x08 (2 rows)

Neither is "every".

What if "bit 1 set" means "at least one bit set"? Then:
- +0x00: no bits set (exception)
- All others: at least one bit set

This fits with the exception note "rev said is at +0x00, every address bit clear". The exception is +0x00 (no bits set), and the rest have at least one bit set.

But "bit 1 set" usually means "bit 1 specifically is set", not "at least one bit set". So the wording is confusing.

Hmm. OK let me also consider another interpretation. "bit 1 set" might be a shorthand for "bit 1 in the bit-position numbering", and the brief is asserting that each said row sits at an offset where bit 1 is set (in the bit-position numbering).

The brief might be using a specific bit-position numbering where "bit 1" is the second bit (1-indexed), which is mask 0x02 in 0-indexed.

For all said rows, mask 0x02 is 0. So the claim is false.

Unless the brief uses 1-indexed bits where bit 1 is mask 0x01. Let me check:

- +0x08: mask 0x01 = 0
- +0x10: mask 0x01 = 0
- +0x18: mask 0x01 = 0
- +0x30: mask 0x01 = 0
- +0x38: mask 0x01 = 0

All clear. Claim is false.

OK so under any reasonable interpretation, the claim "every said row sat at an offset with bit 1 set" is false.

For the said rows, the offsets are 0x00, 0x08, 0x10, 0x18, 0x30, 0x38. In each, "bit 1" (mask 0x02) is 0. So if "bit 1 set" means "bit 1's value is 1", none of the said rows have bit 1 set.

Now let me also verify the brief's claim about what mutations each new row catches.

**sh08b said**: catches a notice suppressed at the first refusal (sh at +0x08). The first refusal at CONTROL should fire its own announce. If the notice is suppressed at +0x08 (e.g., by a bypass or address-bit suppression), then the first refusal's text is not printed at +0x08. The follow-up at +0x07 prints it. Count = 1, but the text is at +0x07 not +0x08.

The row `sh08b said` uses `said(t, 2, 0x8, clean)` - probably counts the text at the offset +0x08. If the text is at +0x07 instead, count = 0. Caught.

**rev said**: similar, for lb at +0x00.

**imask said, istat said**: similar, for lb at INTMASK/INTSTAT.

**time said**: for lb at +0x38.

**dog said**: for lb at +0x30.

These all catch notice suppression at the first refusal's offset.

**lo keeps**: catches a refused store at bit-4-clear offsets that lands. Before this row, all refused stores that read back had bit 4 set. A landing keyed on bit 4 clear was invisible.

**ctl lb v0**: catches `odata = 1` at CONTROL lb (or similar nonzero answer).

**time sd lb v0, time sd lh v0**: catch the register answer at TIME (over a seeded odd TIME).

OK these are meaningful additions.

### Final answer

Let me structure my answer:

**VERDICT**: GAP (the disclosed TIME-landing clause (e) debt is the smallest known floor gap, plus other disclosed gaps).

Actually, the question asks if there's a floor gap. The disclosed TIME-landing gap is one. But it's disclosed as known debt.

Let me re-read the question:
"Is there a floor gap in the 131-row test? The smallest source edit to dev_sgi_ip32.c that undoes part of the fix's behaviour and still scores 131/131, as an exact edit, with the row you expected to catch it and why it cannot."

So the question wants the smallest edit that escapes. The TIME-landing mutation is one such edit. It's the disclosed one.

But the question asks "with the row you expected to catch it". For TIME-landing, there's no row that catches it (that's why it's a gap). The expected row would be "time keeps" (analogous to dog keeps, imask keeps, lo keeps). But it's missing.

So the answer to Q2: the TIME-landing mutation escapes; the expected row "time keeps" is missing.

Let me also think about whether there's a smaller edit that escapes.

Actually, the smallest edit might be different. Let me think about the smallest "undo" of the fix:

1. Remove `goto crime_declined;` (1 line removed). But this is caught.
2. Replace `goto crime_declined;` with `return 0;` (1 word change). Caught.
3. Replace `goto crime_declined;` with nothing (1 line removed). Same as 1.
4. Make the announce only print for some widths (e.g., `if (len == 1)`). Caught by said rows.
5. Make the announce only print for some offsets (e.g., `if (relative_addr != CRIME_TIME)`). Caught by time said.

Hmm. The TIME-landing mutation is in the decline path (not undoing the announce). Let me think of an "undo" specifically.

The fix adds:
- `int warned` field
- `if (!(d->warned & CRIME_W_LEN)) { ... fatal(...); ... }`
- `goto crime_declined;`
- `crime_declined:` label
- `d->reg[CRIME_HARDINT / sizeof(uint64_t)] = preserved_CRIME_HARDINT;`
- `d->reg[CRIME_INTSTAT / sizeof(uint64_t)] = ...`
- `if (writeflag == MEM_READ) memory_writemax64(cpu, data, len, odata);`

An "undo" could be:
- Remove the announce block: `if (!(d->warned & CRIME_W_LEN)) { ... }` removed. Then announce always prints (or never prints, depending on what's left).

If we remove the announce entirely but keep the goto:
```c
goto crime_declined;
```

Then declined accesses don't print. Caught by said rows (no text).

If we remove the goto but keep the announce:
```c
// remove: goto crime_declined;
```

Then declined accesses fall through to switch. Caught by item 3.

If we replace the announce with a no-op:
```c
if (!(d->warned & CRIME_W_LEN)) {
    d->warned |= CRIME_W_LEN;
    // remove: fatal(...);
}
```

Then no text prints. Caught by said rows.

If we remove the latch (d->warned |= CRIME_W_LEN;):
```c
if (!(d->warned & CRIME_W_LEN)) {
    // remove: d->warned |= CRIME_W_LEN;
    fatal(...);
}
```

Then latch never sets. Always prints. Caught by count rows.

These are all caught.

What about removing the goto but keeping the announce and adding a return:
```c
} else {
    if (!(d->warned & CRIME_W_LEN)) { ... fatal(...); }
    return 1; // instead of goto crime_declined;
}
```

Then declined accesses print the announce and return 1. The tail is NOT reached (no HARDINT restore, no INTSTAT recompute, no memory_writemax64).

For declined reads, odata was never set (still 0). The function returns 1 without writing odata to data. So the data buffer is unchanged.

For the test, declined reads expect 0 (the answer). But the data buffer is unchanged, so the answer is whatever was passed in (not 0). Hmm.

Actually wait, let me check how the test reads data. The test uses gxemul to run guest code, then checks the guest's registers (v0, a0, etc.) after the access. The guest code uses `lb`, `lh`, `sb`, `sh` etc., which load/store from CRIME memory.

For a `lb` (load byte) at CRIME: the guest reads the byte. If CRIME returns 0 (decline), the guest's register has 0. If CRIME doesn't write back (data buffer unchanged), the guest's register has whatever was in data.

For `lb`, the data buffer is 1 byte. Initially, it might be 0 (cleared by the caller) or whatever the previous access set. For a declined access, the data buffer might be 0 (cleared) or have a residual value.

The brief mentions: "`goto crime_declined' -> `return 1' is EQUIVALENT on every channel: memory_rw pre-zeroes a device read buffer (#95, src/cpus/memory_rw.c -- `if (writeflag == MEM_READ)' then `memset(data, 0, len);', immediately above `paddr -= mem->devices[i].baseaddr;')".

So memory_rw pre-zeroes the data buffer for reads. So for a declined read, data is 0 even without the tail's memory_writemax64. EQUIVALENT.

For declined writes, data is the input. idata is read from data at the start. The function returns without writing anything (no goto to tail, no register update). The data is unchanged (since idata was read but not used for register update).

For the test, declined writes expect the register to be unchanged (keeps rows). With the mutation, the register IS unchanged (no register update). Same as base. NOT caught by keeps rows.

But wait, the mutation REMOVES the goto. The fall-through to the switch happens. The switch could have side effects (e.g., CONTROL body, INTSTAT/INTMASK/DOG/TIME/default arm).

For declined writes at CONTROL: the switch arm reads idata and acts on it (e.g., fatal if unimplemented). So the side effect (fatal) would fire.

Hmm, so removing the goto and replacing with `return 1` doesn't just return early - the fall-through to the switch happens first.

Unless the mutation is:
```c
} else {
    if (!(d->warned & CRIME_W_LEN)) { ... fatal(...); }
    return 1; // explicit return, no fall-through
}
```

Then no fall-through. Just return 1 after the announce.

The disclosure says this is EQUIVALENT to the goto on every channel. So this mutation passes
